### Log Buffer

The `drgn.helpers.linux.printk` module provides helpers for reading the Linux kernel log buffer.

*class *drgn.helpers.linux.printk.PrintkRecord  
Bases: [`NamedTuple`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.NamedTuple "(in Python v3.14)")

Kernel log record.

text*: [bytes](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#bytes "(in Python v3.14)")*  
Message text.

facility*: [int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")*  
*[syslog(3)](http://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man3/syslog.3.html)* facility.

level*: [int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")*  
Log level.

seq*: [int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")*  
Sequence number.

timestamp*: [int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")*  
Timestamp in nanoseconds.

caller_tid*: [Optional](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Optional "(in Python v3.14)")\[[int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")\]*  
Thread ID of thread that logged this record, if available.

This is available if the message was logged from task context and if the kernel saves the `printk()` caller ID.

As of Linux 5.10, the kernel always saves the caller ID. From Linux 5.1 through 5.9, it is saved only if the kernel was compiled with `CONFIG_PRINTK_CALLER`. Before that, it is never saved.

caller_cpu*: [Optional](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Optional "(in Python v3.14)")\[[int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")\]*  
Processor ID of CPU that logged this record, if available.

This is available only if the message was logged when not in task context (e.g., in an interrupt handler) and if the kernel saves the `printk()` caller ID.

See [`caller_tid`](#drgn.helpers.linux.printk.PrintkRecord.caller_tid "drgn.helpers.linux.printk.PrintkRecord.caller_tid") for when the kernel saves the caller ID.

continuation*: [bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)")*  
Whether this record is a continuation of a previous record.

context*: [Dict](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Dict "(in Python v3.14)")\[[bytes](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#bytes "(in Python v3.14)"), [bytes](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#bytes "(in Python v3.14)")\]*  
Additional metadata for the message.

See the [`/dev/kmsg` documentation](https://www.kernel.org/doc/Documentation/ABI/testing/dev-kmsg) for an explanation of the keys and values.

drgn.helpers.linux.printk.get_dmesg(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*, *\**, *timestamps: [Union](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Union "(in Python v3.14)")\[[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)"), [Literal](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Literal "(in Python v3.14)")\['human'\]\] = True*) → [bytes](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#bytes "(in Python v3.14)")  
Get the contents of the kernel log buffer formatted like *[dmesg(1)](http://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man1/dmesg.1.html)*.

If you just want to print the log buffer, use [`print_dmesg()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.printk.print_dmesg "drgn.helpers.linux.printk.print_dmesg").

The format of each line is:

    [   timestamp] message

If you need to format the log buffer differently, use [`get_printk_records()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.printk.get_printk_records "drgn.helpers.linux.printk.get_printk_records") and format it yourself.

Parameters:  
- **prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

- **timestamps** – How to format timestamps. If `True`, timestamps are formatted in decimal seconds. If `False`, timestamps are omitted. If `"human"`, timestamps are formatted as human-readable dates and times, which are only correct for messages printed since the last suspend/resume.

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.printk.print_dmesg(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*, *\**, *timestamps: [Union](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Union "(in Python v3.14)")\[[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)"), [Literal](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Literal "(in Python v3.14)")\['human'\]\] = True*, *file: [Optional](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Optional "(in Python v3.14)")\[SupportsWrite\[[str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)")\]\] = None*) → [None](https://docs.python.org/3/library/constants.html#None "(in Python v3.14)")  
Print the contents of the kernel log buffer.

    >>> print_dmesg()
    [    0.000000] Linux version 6.8.0-vmtest28.1default (drgn@drgn) (x86_64-linux-gcc (GCC) 12.2.0, GNU ld (GNU Binutils) 2.39) #1 SMP PREEMPT_DYNAMIC Mon Mar 11 06:38:45 UTC 2024
    [    0.000000] Command line: rootfstype=9p rootflags=trans=virtio,cache=loose,msize=1048576 ro console=ttyS0,115200 panic=-1 crashkernel=256M init=/tmp/drgn-vmtest-rudzppeo/init
    [    0.000000] BIOS-provided physical RAM map:
    ...

Parameters:  
- **prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

- **timestamps** – How to format timestamps. See [`get_dmesg()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.printk.get_dmesg "drgn.helpers.linux.printk.get_dmesg").

- **file** – File to print to. Defaults to [`sys.stdout`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/sys.html#sys.stdout "(in Python v3.14)").

drgn.helpers.linux.rbtree.rb_find(*type: [Union](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Union "(in Python v3.14)")\[[str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)"), [drgn.Type](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Type "drgn.Type")\]*, *root: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*, *member: [str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)")*, *key: KeyType*, *cmp: [Callable](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Callable "(in Python v3.14)")\[\[KeyType, [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")\], [int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")\]*) → [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")  
Find an entry in a red-black tree given a key and a comparator function.

Note that this function does not have an analogue in the Linux kernel source code, as tree searches are all open-coded.

Parameters:  
- **type** – Entry type.

- **root** – `struct`` ``rb_root`` ``*`

- **member** – Name of `struct`` ``rb_node` member in entry type.

- **key** – Key to find.

- **cmp** – Callback taking key and entry that returns \< 0 if the key is less than the entry, \> 0 if the key is greater than the entry, and 0 if the key matches the entry.

Returns:  
`type`` ``*` found entry, or `NULL` if not found.

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.rbtree.validate_rbtree(*type: [Union](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Union "(in Python v3.14)")\[[str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)"), [drgn.Type](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Type "drgn.Type")\]*, *root: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*, *member: [str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)")*, *cmp: [Callable](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Callable "(in Python v3.14)")\[\[[drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object"), [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")\], [int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")\]*, *allow_equal: [bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)")*) → [None](https://docs.python.org/3/library/constants.html#None "(in Python v3.14)")  
Validate a red-black tree.

This checks that:

1.  The tree is a valid binary search tree ordered according to *cmp*.

2.  If *allow_equal* is `False`, there are no nodes that compare equal according to *cmp*.

3.  The `rb_parent` pointers are consistent.

4.  The red-black tree requirements are satisfied: the root node is black, no red node has a red child, and every path from any node to any of its descendant leaf nodes goes through the same number of black nodes.

Parameters:  
- **type** – Entry type.

- **root** – `struct`` ``rb_root`` ``*`

- **member** – Name of `struct`` ``rb_node` member in entry type.

- **cmp** – Callback taking two `type`` ``*` entry objects that returns \< 0 if the first entry is less than the second entry, \> 0 if the first entry is greater than the second entry, and 0 if they are equal.

- **allow_equal** – Whether the tree may contain entries that compare equal to each other.

Raises:  
[**ValidationError**](#drgn.helpers.ValidationError "drgn.helpers.ValidationError") – if the tree is invalid

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.rbtree.validate_rbtree_inorder_for_each_entry(*type: [Union](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Union "(in Python v3.14)")\[[str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)"), [drgn.Type](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Type "drgn.Type")\]*, *root: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*, *member: [str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)")*, *cmp: [Callable](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Callable "(in Python v3.14)")\[\[[drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object"), [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")\], [int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")\]*, *allow_equal: [bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)")*) → [Iterator](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Iterator "(in Python v3.14)")\[[drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")\]  
Like [`rbtree_inorder_for_each_entry()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.rbtree.rbtree_inorder_for_each_entry "drgn.helpers.linux.rbtree.rbtree_inorder_for_each_entry"), but validates the red-black tree like [`validate_rbtree()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.rbtree.validate_rbtree "drgn.helpers.linux.rbtree.validate_rbtree") while iterating.

Parameters:  
- **type** – Entry type.

- **root** – `struct`` ``rb_root`` ``*`

- **member** – Name of `struct`` ``rb_node` member in entry type.

- **cmp** – Callback taking two `type`` ``*` entry objects that returns \< 0 if the first entry is less than the second entry, \> 0 if the first entry is greater than the second entry, and 0 if they are equal.

- **allow_equal** – Whether the tree may contain entries that compare equal to each other.

Raises:  
[**ValidationError**](#drgn.helpers.ValidationError "drgn.helpers.ValidationError") – if the tree is invalid

### Resource Limits

The `drgn.helpers.linux.resource` module provides helpers for resource limits (rlimit).

drgn.helpers.linux.resource.task_rlimits(*task: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [Dict](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Dict "(in Python v3.14)")\[[str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)"), 'Rlimit'\]  
Get the resource limits on a given task.

    >>> task_rlimits(find_task(os.getpid()))
    {'CPU': Rlimit(cur=None, max=None), 'FSIZE': Rlimit(cur=None, max=None), ...}
    >>> task_rlimits(find_task(os.getpid()))["STACK"].cur
    8388608

Returns:  
Dictionary mapping resource name (“NOFILE”, “CORE”, etc.) to [`Rlimit`](#drgn.helpers.linux.resource.Rlimit "drgn.helpers.linux.resource.Rlimit").

&nbsp;

*class *drgn.helpers.linux.resource.Rlimit  
Bases: [`NamedTuple`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.NamedTuple "(in Python v3.14)")

Resource limit.

cur*: [Optional](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Optional "(in Python v3.14)")\[[int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")\]*  
Soft limit, or `None` if unlimited.

max*: [Optional](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Optional "(in Python v3.14)")\[[int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")\]*  
Hard limit, or `None` if unlimited.

drgn.helpers.linux.sched.cfs_rq_for_each_entity(*cfs_rq: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [Iterator](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Iterator "(in Python v3.14)")\[[Tuple](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Tuple "(in Python v3.14)")\[[drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object"), [int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)"), [bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)"), [bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)")\]\]  
Iterate over all entities on a fair scheduler runqueue (recursively).

Task groups are visited before their children (i.e., this does a pre-order traversal).

Parameters:  
**cfs_rq** – `struct`` ``cfs_rq`` ``*`

Returns:  
Iterator of (`struct`` ``sched_entity`` ``*`, `depth`, `is_curr`, `is_task`) tuples.

`depth` is the depth of the entity relative to the runqueue (e.g., direct children have depth 0, entities in descendant task groups have depth \> 0).

`is_curr` is whether the entity is the current entity on its runqueue.

If `is_task` is `True`, then the entity is a task, which can be obtained with [`sched_entity_to_task()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.sched.sched_entity_to_task "drgn.helpers.linux.sched.sched_entity_to_task"). Otherwise, it is a task group.

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.sched.task_since_last_arrival_ns(*task: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")  
Get the difference between the runqueue timestamp when a task last started running and the current runqueue timestamp.

This is approximately the time that the task has been in its current status (running, queued, or blocked). However, if a CPU is either idle or running the same task for a long time, then the timestamps will not be accurate.

This is only supported if the kernel was compiled with `CONFIG_SCHEDSTATS` or `CONFIG_TASK_DELAY_ACCT`.

Parameters:  
**task** – `struct`` ``task_struct`` ``*`

Returns:  
Duration in nanoseconds.

&nbsp;

Parameters:  
**task** – `struct`` ``task_struct`` ``*`

Get a mapping from signal names to numbers on this kernel.

    >>> signal_names()
    {'SIGHUP': 1, 'SIGINT': 2, 'SIGQUIT': 3, ...}

If there are multiple names for the same number (e.g., `SIGCHLD` and `SIGCLD`), then the preferred name comes first.

Note that `SIGRTMIN` and `SIGRTMAX` are dynamic in userspace, so the values used in the kernel may differ from those in userspace.

Parameters:  
**prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

### Slab Allocator

The `drgn.helpers.linux.slab` module provides helpers for working with the Linux slab allocator.

Warning

Beware of slab merging when using these helpers. See [`slab_cache_is_merged()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.slab.slab_cache_is_merged "drgn.helpers.linux.slab.slab_cache_is_merged").

drgn.helpers.linux.slab.slab_cache_objects_per_slab(*slab_cache: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")  
Get the number of objects in each slab of the given slab cache.

This is only applicable to the SLUB and SLAB allocators; SLOB is not supported.

Parameters:  
**slab_cache** – `struct`` ``kmem_cache`` ``*`

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.slab.slab_cache_pages_per_slab(*slab_cache: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")  
Get the number of pages allocated for each slab of the given slab cache.

This is only applicable to the SLUB and SLAB allocators; SLOB is not supported.

Parameters:  
**slab_cache** – `struct`` ``kmem_cache`` ``*`

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.slab.slab_cache_order(*slab_cache: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")  
Get the allocation order (i.e., base 2 logarithm of the number of pages) for each slab of the given slab cache.

This is only applicable to the SLUB and SLAB allocators; SLOB is not supported.

    >>> 1 << slab_cache_order(slab_cache) == slab_cache_pages_per_slab(slab_cache)
    True

Parameters:  
**slab_cache** – `struct`` ``kmem_cache`` ``*`

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.slab.slab_cache_is_merged(*slab_cache: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)")  
Return whether a slab cache has been merged with any other slab caches.

Unless configured otherwise, the kernel may merge slab caches of similar sizes together. See the [SLUB users guide](https://docs.kernel.org/vm/slub.html#slab-merging) and `slab_merge`/`slab_nomerge` in the [kernel parameters documentation](https://www.kernel.org/doc/Documentation/admin-guide/kernel-parameters.txt).

This can cause confusion, as only the name of the first cache will be found, and objects of different types will be mixed in the same slab cache.

For example, suppose that we have two types, `struct`` ``foo` and `struct`` ``bar`, which have the same size but are otherwise unrelated. If the kernel creates a slab cache named `foo` for `struct`` ``foo`, then another slab cache named `bar` for `struct`` ``bar`, then slab cache `foo` will be reused instead of creating another cache for `bar`. So the following will fail:

    find_slab_cache("bar")

And the following will also return `struct`` ``bar`` ``*` objects errantly casted to `struct`` ``foo`` ``*`:

    slab_cache_for_each_allocated_object(find_slab_cache("foo"), "struct foo")

Unfortunately, these issues are difficult to work around generally, so one must be prepared to handle them on a case-by-case basis (e.g., by looking up the slab cache by its variable name and by checking that members of the structure make sense for the expected type).

Parameters:  
**slab_cache** – `struct`` ``kmem_cache`` ``*`

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.slab.get_slab_cache_aliases(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*) → [Dict](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Dict "(in Python v3.14)")\[[str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)"), [str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)")\]  
Return a dict mapping slab cache name to the cache it was merged with.

The SLAB and SLUB subsystems can merge caches with similar settings and object sizes, as described in the documentation of [`slab_cache_is_merged()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.slab.slab_cache_is_merged "drgn.helpers.linux.slab.slab_cache_is_merged"). In some cases, the information about which caches were merged is lost, but in other cases, we can reconstruct the info. This function reconstructs the mapping, but requires that the kernel is configured with `CONFIG_SLUB` and `CONFIG_SYSFS`.

The returned dict maps from original cache name, to merged cache name. You can use this mapping to discover the correct cache to lookup via [`find_slab_cache()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.slab.find_slab_cache "drgn.helpers.linux.slab.find_slab_cache"). The dict contains an entry only for caches which were merged into a cache of a different name.

    >>> cache_to_merged = get_slab_cache_aliases()
    >>> cache_to_merged["dnotify_struct"]
    'avc_xperms_data'
    >>> "avc_xperms_data" in cache_to_merged
    False
    >>> find_slab_cache("dnotify_struct") is None
    True
    >>> find_slab_cache("avc_xperms_data") is None
    False

Warning:  
This function will only work on kernels which are built with `CONFIG_SLUB` and `CONFIG_SYSFS` enabled.

Parameters:  
**prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

Returns:  
Mapping of slab cache name to final merged name

Raises:  
[**LookupError**](https://docs.python.org/3/library/exceptions.html#LookupError "(in Python v3.14)") – If the helper fails because the debugged kernel doesn’t have the required configuration

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.slab.for_each_slab_cache(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*) → [Iterator](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Iterator "(in Python v3.14)")\[[drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")\]

Parameters:

*class *drgn.helpers.linux.slab.SlabCacheUsage

drgn.helpers.linux.slab.slab_cache_for_each_allocated_object(*slab_cache: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*, *type: [Union](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Union "(in Python v3.14)")\[[str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)"), [drgn.Type](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Type "drgn.Type")\]*) → [Iterator](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Iterator "(in Python v3.14)")\[[drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")\]  
Iterate over all allocated objects in a given slab cache.

    >>> dentry_cache = find_slab_cache("dentry")
    >>> next(slab_cache_for_each_allocated_object(dentry_cache, "struct dentry"))
    *(struct dentry *)0xffff905e41404000 = {
        ...
    }

drgn.helpers.linux.slab.slab_object_info(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*, *addr: [drgn.IntegerLike](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.IntegerLike "drgn.IntegerLike")*) → [Optional](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Optional "(in Python v3.14)")\[[SlabObjectInfo](#drgn.helpers.linux.slab.SlabObjectInfo "drgn.helpers.linux.slab.SlabObjectInfo")\]  
Get information about an address if it is in a slab object.

    >>> ptr = find_task(1).comm.address_of_()
    >>> info = slab_object_info(ptr)
    >>> info
    SlabObjectInfo(slab_cache=Object(prog, 'struct kmem_cache *', address=0xffffdb93c0045e18), slab=Object(prog, 'struct slab *', value=0xffffdb93c0045e00), address=0xffffa2bf81178000, allocated=True)

Note that [`SlabObjectInfo.address`](#drgn.helpers.linux.slab.SlabObjectInfo.address "drgn.helpers.linux.slab.SlabObjectInfo.address") is the start address of the object, which may be less than *addr* if *addr* points to a member inside of the object:

    >>> ptr.value_() - info.address
    1496
    >>> offsetof(prog.type("struct task_struct"), "comm")
    1496

Note that SLOB does not store enough information to identify slab objects, so if the kernel is configured to use SLOB, then [`SlabObjectInfo.slab_cache`](#drgn.helpers.linux.slab.SlabObjectInfo.slab_cache "drgn.helpers.linux.slab.SlabObjectInfo.slab_cache") will always be `NULL` and [`SlabObjectInfo.address`](#drgn.helpers.linux.slab.SlabObjectInfo.address "drgn.helpers.linux.slab.SlabObjectInfo.address") will always be 0. Additionally, for allocations of at least one page, SLOB allocates pages directly, so this will return `None`.

Parameters:  
- **prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

- **addr** – `void`` ``*`

Returns:  
[`SlabObjectInfo`](#drgn.helpers.linux.slab.SlabObjectInfo "drgn.helpers.linux.slab.SlabObjectInfo") if *addr* is in a slab object, or `None` if not.

&nbsp;

*class *drgn.helpers.linux.slab.SlabObjectInfo

slab_cache*: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*

SLOB does not store enough information to find this, so if the kernel is configured to use SLOB, then this will always be `NULL`.

Since Linux v5.17, this is a `struct`` ``slab`` ``*`. Before that, it is a `struct`` ``page`` ``*`.

address*: [int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)")*

SLOB does not store enough information to find this, so if the kernel is configured to use SLOB, then this will always be 0.

allocated*: [Optional](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Optional "(in Python v3.14)")\[[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)")\]*  
`True` if the object is allocated, `False` if it is free, or `None` if not known because the slab cache is corrupted or the kernel is configured to use SLOB.

### Stack

The `drgn.helpers.linux.stack` module contains helpers for working with Linux kernel stacks. These helpers can provide additional information specific to the kernel.

*class *drgn.helpers.linux.stack.StackKind  
Bases: [`enum.Enum`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/enum.html#enum.Enum "(in Python v3.14)")

A `StackKind` represents a kind of context associated with a stack.

USER  
Stack frames associated with userspace

TASK  
Stack frames associated with the kernel’s thread stack

IRQ  
Stack frames associated with IRQ handlers

SOFTIRQ  
Stack frames associated with softirqs

NMI  
Stack frames associated with NMI handlers (arch-specific)

EXCEPTION  
Stack frames associated with other exceptions (arch-specific)

UNKNOWN  
Stack frames which could not be categorized

&nbsp;

*class *drgn.helpers.linux.stack.StackSegment  
A subset of a stack trace associated with a context.

kind*: [StackKind](#drgn.helpers.linux.stack.StackKind "drgn.helpers.linux.stack.StackKind")*  
The kind of stack associated with this segment

frames*: [Sequence](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Sequence "(in Python v3.14)")\[[drgn.StackFrame](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.StackFrame "drgn.StackFrame")\]*  
Stack frames that are part of the segment

&nbsp;

*class *drgn.helpers.linux.stack.LinuxKernelStack  
A stack trace broken into segments associated with execution contexts.

Stack traces for kernel programs may span many execution contexts, each one interrupting the previous one. For instance, a user task may be interrupted by the kernel handling its system call, which could in turn be interrupted by a hardware interrupt. A `LinuxKernelStack` groups the frames of a stack trace into [`StackSegments`](#drgn.helpers.linux.stack.StackSegment "drgn.helpers.linux.stack.StackSegment"), each of which is associated with a [`StackKind`](#drgn.helpers.linux.stack.StackKind "drgn.helpers.linux.stack.StackKind"). It may also be iterated and indexed like a normal [`StackTrace`](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.StackTrace "drgn.StackTrace").

prog*: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*  
The program associated with the stack trace

segments*: [Sequence](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Sequence "(in Python v3.14)")\[[StackSegment](#drgn.helpers.linux.stack.StackSegment "drgn.helpers.linux.stack.StackSegment")\]*  
Segments of the stack trace

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.stack.kernel_stack_trace(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*, *task_arg: [Union](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Union "(in Python v3.14)")\[[drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object"), [drgn.IntegerLike](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.IntegerLike "drgn.IntegerLike")\]*) → [LinuxKernelStack](#drgn.helpers.linux.stack.LinuxKernelStack "drgn.helpers.linux.stack.LinuxKernelStack")  
Create a stack trace and return a categorized [`LinuxKernelStack`](#drgn.helpers.linux.stack.LinuxKernelStack "drgn.helpers.linux.stack.LinuxKernelStack").

This function creates a stack trace, breaks it into segments, and categorizes each segment. The process is best-effort. Not all stack kinds can be segmented or recognized, as the process is architecture and version specific.

Parameters:  
- **prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

- **task** – a PID or `struct`` ``task_struct`` ``*` to unwind

Returns:  
a separated, annotated stack

Get the total number of swap pages and the number of free swap pages on all swap devices.

    >>> usage = swap_total_usage()
    >>> usage
    PageUsage(pages=2097151, free_pages=1704798)
    >>> usage.used_pages
    392353

Parameters:  
**prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

&nbsp;

Parameters:  
**prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

### Sysfs

The `drgn.helpers.linux.sysfs` module provides helpers for working with the sysfs interface built on top of kernfs.

drgn.helpers.linux.sysfs.sysfs_lookup_node(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*, *path: [str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)")*) → [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")  
Look up a `struct`` ``kernfs_node`` ``*` for a given sysfs path.

The path may be provided either relative to `/sys` (e.g., `devices/system/cpu`) or as an absolute path beginning with `/sys`.

If the path is empty or refers to `/sys` itself, this returns the sysfs root node.

Parameters:  
- **prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

- **path** – Sysfs path (absolute or relative to `/sys`)

Returns:  
`struct`` ``kernfs_node`` ``*` or `NULL`

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.sysfs.sysfs_lookup_kobject(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*, *path: [str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)")*) → [Optional](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Optional "(in Python v3.14)")\[[drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")\]  
Look up the `struct`` ``kobject`` ``*` corresponding to a sysfs path.

If the path refers to a directory, this returns `kn->priv`. If the path refers to a file (attribute), this returns the parent kobject (i.e., the parent node’s `priv`)

Parameters:  
- **prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

- **path** – Sysfs path (absolute or relative to `/sys`)

Returns:  
`struct`` ``kobject`` ``*` or `NULL`

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.sysfs.sysfs_lookup(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*, *path: [str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)")*) → [Optional](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Optional "(in Python v3.14)")\[[drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")\]  
Look up the object represented by a sysfs path.

Resolve the `struct`` ``kobject` corresponding to a sysfs entry and, when possible, return the kernel structure which contains that kobject.

Note: This may return more specific types for other cases in the future.

Depending on the kobject type, this helper may return:

- `struct`` ``device`` ``*`

- `struct`` ``class`` ``*`

- `struct`` ``bus_type`` ``*`

- `struct`` ``device_driver`` ``*`

- `struct`` ``module_kobject`` ``*`

If the kobject type does not correspond to a known container structure, the function returns the `struct`` ``kobject`` ``*`.

Parameters:  
- **prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

- **path** – Sysfs path (absolute or relative to `/sys`)

Returns:  
Corresponding container structure pointer or `struct`` ``kobject`` ``*` or `NULL`

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.sysfs.sysfs_listdir(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*, *path: [str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)")*) → [List](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.List "(in Python v3.14)")\[[bytes](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#bytes "(in Python v3.14)")\]  
List the children of a sysfs directory.

Parameters:  
- **prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

- **path** – Sysfs directory path (absolute or relative to `/sys`)

Returns:  
List of child entry names

Raises:  
[**ValueError**](https://docs.python.org/3/library/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.14)") – If path is not found or not a directory

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.sysfs.kobject_path(*kobj: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [bytes](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#bytes "(in Python v3.14)")  
Return the sysfs path corresponding to a `struct`` ``kobject`` ``*`.

Parameters:  
**kobj** – `struct`` ``kobject`` ``*`

Returns:  
Absolute path in sysfs

### Timekeeping

The `drgn.helpers.linux.timekeeping` module provides helpers for timestamps.

Note

In core dumps, it is only possible to recover coarse timestamps, which are only updated once per tick (~1-10 ms). Therefore, only the `ktime_get_seconds()` and `ktime_get_coarse_ns()` families of functions are provided. These functions can also race with timekeeping updates and return a value with an error of up to 1 second.

drgn.helpers.linux.timekeeping.ktime_to_ns(*kt: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")  
Convert a `ktime_t` to nanoseconds.

Parameters:  
**kt** – `ktime_t`

Returns:  
`s64`

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.timekeeping.ktime_get_seconds(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*) → [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")  
Get the seconds component of the monotonic time (`CLOCK_MONOTONIC_COARSE`).

Parameters:  
**prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

Returns:  
`time64_t`

&nbsp;

### Timers

The `drgn.helpers.linux.timer` module provides helpers for kernel timers, including the timer wheel and high-resolution timers (“hrtimers”).

drgn.helpers.linux.timer.timer_base_names(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*) → [Sequence](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Sequence "(in Python v3.14)")\[[str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)")\]  
Get the names of the timer wheel bases used on this kernel.

This depends on the kernel version and configuration. For example:

    >>> timer_base_names()
    ('BASE_LOCAL', 'BASE_GLOBAL', 'BASE_DEF')

means that each CPU has 3 timer bases: `BASE_LOCAL` (0), `BASE_GLOBAL` (1), and `BASE_DEF` (2).

Parameters:  
**prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.timer.timer_base_for_each(*base: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [Iterator](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Iterator "(in Python v3.14)")\[[drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")\]  
Iterate over every timer on a timer base.

    for cpu in for_each_online_cpu():
        for base in per_cpu(prog["timer_bases"], cpu):
            for timer in timer_base_for_each(base.address_of_()):
                ...

Parameters:  
**base** – `struct`` ``timer_base`` ``*`

Returns:  
Iterator of `struct`` ``timer_list`` ``*` objects.

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.timer.hrtimer_clock_base_for_each(*clock_base: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [Iterator](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Iterator "(in Python v3.14)")\[[drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")\]  
Iterate over every high-resolution timer on an hrtimer clock base.

    for cpu in for_each_online_cpu():
        for cpu_base in per_cpu(prog["hrtimer_bases"], cpu):
            for clock_base in cpu_base.clock_base:
                for hrtimer in hrtimer_clock_base_for_each(clock_base.address_of_()):
                    ...

Parameters:  
**clock_base** – `struct`` ``hrtimer_clock_base`` ``*`

Returns:  
Iterator of `struct`` ``hrtimer`` ``*` objects.

### Wait Queues

The `drgn.helpers.linux.wait` module provides helpers for working with wait queues (`wait_queue_head_t` and `wait_queue_entry_t`) from [`include/linux/wait.h`](https://git.kernel.org/pub/scm/linux/kernel/git/torvalds/linux.git/tree/include/linux/wait.h).

Note

Since Linux 4.13, entries in a wait queue have type `wait_queue_entry_t`. Before that, the type was named `wait_queue_t`.

drgn.helpers.linux.wait.waitqueue_active(*wq: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)")  
Return whether a wait queue has any waiters.

Parameters:  
**wq** – `wait_queue_head_t`` ``*`

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.wait.waitqueue_for_each_entry(*wq: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [Iterator](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Iterator "(in Python v3.14)")\[[drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")\]  
Iterate over all entries in a wait queue.

Parameters:  
**wq** – `wait_queue_head_t`` ``*`

Returns:  
Iterator of `wait_queue_entry_t`` ``*` or `wait_queue_t`` ``*` objects depending on the kernel version.

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.wait.waitqueue_for_each_task(*wq: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [Iterator](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Iterator "(in Python v3.14)")\[[drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")\]  
Iterate over all tasks waiting on a wait queue.

Warning

This comes from `wait_queue_entry_t::private`, which usually stores a task. However, some wait queue entries store a different pointer type, in which case this will return garbage.

Parameters:  
**wq** – `wait_queue_head_t`` ``*`

Returns:  
Iterator of `struct`` ``task_struct`` ``*` objects.

### XArrays

The `drgn.helpers.linux.xarray` module provides helpers for working with the [XArray](https://docs.kernel.org/core-api/xarray.html) data structure from [`include/linux/xarray.h`](https://git.kernel.org/pub/scm/linux/kernel/git/torvalds/linux.git/tree/include/linux/xarray.h).

Note

XArrays were introduced in Linux 4.20 as a replacement for [radix trees](#radix-trees). To make it easier to work with data structures that were changed from a radix tree to an XArray (like `struct`` ``address_space::i_pages`), drgn treats XArrays and radix trees interchangeably in some cases.

Specifically, [`xa_load()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_load "drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_load") is equivalent to [`radix_tree_lookup()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.radixtree.radix_tree_lookup "drgn.helpers.linux.radixtree.radix_tree_lookup"), and [`xa_for_each()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_for_each "drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_for_each") is equivalent to [`radix_tree_for_each()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.radixtree.radix_tree_for_each "drgn.helpers.linux.radixtree.radix_tree_for_each"), except that the radix tree helpers assume `advanced=False`. (Therefore, [`xa_load()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_load "drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_load") and [`xa_for_each()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_for_each "drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_for_each") also accept a `struct`` ``radix_tree_root`` ``*`, and [`radix_tree_lookup()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.radixtree.radix_tree_lookup "drgn.helpers.linux.radixtree.radix_tree_lookup") and [`radix_tree_for_each()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.radixtree.radix_tree_for_each "drgn.helpers.linux.radixtree.radix_tree_for_each") also accept a `struct`` ``xarray`` ``*`.)

drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_load(*xa: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*, *index: [drgn.IntegerLike](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.IntegerLike "drgn.IntegerLike")*, *\**, *advanced: [bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)") = False*) → [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")  
Look up the entry at a given index in an XArray.

    >>> entry = xa_load(inode.i_mapping.i_pages.address_of_(), 2)
    >>> cast("struct page *", entry)
    *(struct page *)0xffffed6980306f40 = {
        ...
    }

Parameters:  
- **xa** – `struct`` ``xarray`` ``*`

- **index** – Entry index.

- **advanced** – Whether to return nodes only visible to the XArray advanced API. If `False`, zero entries (see [`xa_is_zero()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_is_zero "drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_is_zero")) will be returned as `NULL`.

Returns:  
`void`` ``*` found entry, or `NULL` if not found.

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_for_each(*xa: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*, *\**, *advanced: [bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)") = False*) → [Iterator](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Iterator "(in Python v3.14)")\[[Tuple](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Tuple "(in Python v3.14)")\[[int](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)"), [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")\]\]  
Iterate over all of the entries in an XArray.

    >>> for index, entry in xa_for_each(inode.i_mapping.i_pages.address_of_()):
    ...     print(index, entry)
    ...
    0 (void *)0xffffed6980356140
    1 (void *)0xffffed6980306f80
    2 (void *)0xffffed6980306f40
    3 (void *)0xffffed6980355b40

Parameters:  
- **xa** – `struct`` ``xarray`` ``*`

- **advanced** – Whether to return nodes only visible to the XArray advanced API. If `False`, zero entries (see [`xa_is_zero()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_is_zero "drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_is_zero")) will be skipped.

Returns:  
Iterator of (index, `void`` ``*`) tuples.

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_is_value(*entry: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)")  
Return whether an XArray entry is a value.

See [`xa_to_value()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_to_value "drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_to_value").

Parameters:  
**entry** – `void`` ``*`

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_to_value(*entry: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")  
Return the value in an XArray entry.

In addition to pointers, XArrays can store integers between 0 and `LONG_MAX`. If [`xa_is_value()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_is_value "drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_is_value") returns `True`, use this to get the stored integer.

    >>> entry = xa_load(xa, 9)
    >>> entry
    (void *)0xc9
    >>> xa_is_value(entry)
    True
    >>> xa_to_value(entry)
    (unsigned long)100

Parameters:  
**entry** – `void`` ``*`

Returns:  
`unsigned`` ``long`

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_is_zero(*entry: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)")  
Return whether an XArray entry is a “zero” entry.

A zero entry is an entry that was reserved but is not present. These are only visible to the XArray advanced API, so they are only returned by [`xa_load()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_load "drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_load") and [`xa_for_each()`](#drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_for_each "drgn.helpers.linux.xarray.xa_for_each") when `advanced`` ``=`` ``True`.

    >>> entry = xa_load(xa, 10, advanced=True)
    >>> entry
    (void *)0x406
    >>> xa_is_zero(entry)
    True
    >>> xa_load(xa, 10)
    (void *)0

Parameters:  
**entry** – `void`` ``*`

## Experimental

The `drgn.helpers.experimental` package contains experimental helpers with no stability guarantees. They may change, move to another package, or be removed. They are not automatically imported by the CLI.

### Kmodify

The `drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify` module provides experimental helpers for modifying the state of the running kernel. This works by loading a temporary kernel module, so the kernel must support loadable kernel modules (`CONFIG_MODULES=y`) and allow loading unsigned modules (`CONFIG_MODULE_SIG_FORCE=n`). It is currently only implemented for x86-64.

Warning

These helpers are powerful but **extremely** dangerous. Use them with care.

drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.write_memory(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*, *address: [drgn.IntegerLike](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.IntegerLike "drgn.IntegerLike")*, *value: [bytes](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#bytes "(in Python v3.14)")*) → [None](https://docs.python.org/3/library/constants.html#None "(in Python v3.14)")  
Write a byte string to kernel memory.

    >>> os.uname().sysname
    'Linux'
    >>> write_memory(prog["init_uts_ns"].name.sysname.address_, b"Lol\0")
    >>> os.uname().sysname
    'Lol'

Warning

This attempts to detect writes to bad addresses and raise a [`FaultError`](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.FaultError "drgn.FaultError"), but this is best-effort and may still crash the kernel. Writing bad data can of course also cause a crash when the data is used. Additionally, this is not atomic, so the data may be accessed while it is partially written.

Parameters:  
- **prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

- **address** – Address to write to.

- **value** – Byte string to write.

Raises:  
[**FaultError**](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.FaultError "drgn.FaultError") – if the address cannot be written to

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.set_bit(*nr: [drgn.IntegerLike](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.IntegerLike "drgn.IntegerLike")*, *bitmap: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [None](https://docs.python.org/3/library/constants.html#None "(in Python v3.14)")  
Atomically set a bit in kernel memory.

Warning

This attempts to detect bad addresses and raise a [`FaultError`](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.FaultError "drgn.FaultError"), but this is best-effort and may still crash the kernel. In particular, this function will crash the kernel if the target address is read-only. Writing an invalid bit can of course also cause a crash when the data is used.

Parameters:  
- **nr** – Bit number.

- **bitmap** – `unsigned`` ``long`` ``*`

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.clear_bit(*nr: [drgn.IntegerLike](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.IntegerLike "drgn.IntegerLike")*, *bitmap: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*) → [None](https://docs.python.org/3/library/constants.html#None "(in Python v3.14)")  
Atomically clear a bit in kernel memory.

Warning

The warnings about [`set_bit()`](#drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.set_bit "drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.set_bit") also apply to `clear_bit()`.

Parameters:  
- **nr** – Bit number.

- **bitmap** – `unsigned`` ``long`` ``*`

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.write_object(*object: [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")*, *value: [Any](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Any "(in Python v3.14)")*, *\**, *dereference: [Optional](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Optional "(in Python v3.14)")\[[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)")\] = None*) → [None](https://docs.python.org/3/library/constants.html#None "(in Python v3.14)")  
Write to an object in kernel memory.

    >>> os.system("uptime -p")
    up 12 minutes
    >>> write_object(prog["init_time_ns"].offsets.boottime.tv_sec, 1000000000)
    >>> os.system("uptime -p")
    up 3 decades, 1 year, 37 weeks, 1 hour, 59 minutes

Bit fields are currently only supported if they are either byte-aligned or a single bit. Writes to a bit field are atomic with respect to other bit fields.

Warning

The warnings about [`write_memory()`](#drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.write_memory "drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.write_memory") and [`set_bit()`](#drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.set_bit "drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.set_bit") also apply to `write_object()`.

Parameters:  
- **object** – Object to write to.

- **value** – Value to write. This may be an [`Object`](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object") or a Python value. Either way, it will be converted to the type of *object*.

- **dereference** – If *object* is a pointer, whether to dereference it. If `True`, then write to the object pointed to by *object* (`*ptr`` ``=`` ``value`). If `False`, then write to the pointer itself (`ptr`` ``=`` ``value`). This is a common source of confusion, so it is required if *object* is a pointer.

Raises:  
- [**ValueError**](https://docs.python.org/3/library/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.14)") – if *object* is not a reference object (i.e., its address is not known)

- [**TypeError**](https://docs.python.org/3/library/exceptions.html#TypeError "(in Python v3.14)") – if *object* is a pointer and *dereference* is not given

- [**TypeError**](https://docs.python.org/3/library/exceptions.html#TypeError "(in Python v3.14)") – if *object* is not a pointer and *dereference* is `True`

- [**NotImplementedError**](https://docs.python.org/3/library/exceptions.html#NotImplementedError "(in Python v3.14)") – if *object* is a bit field that is not byte-aligned or a single bit

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.call_function(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*, *func: [Union](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Union "(in Python v3.14)")\[[str](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)"), [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")\]*, *\*args: [Any](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Any "(in Python v3.14)")*) → [drgn.Object](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")  
Call a function in the kernel.

    >>> task = find_task(99)
    >>> if task:
    ...     call_function("wake_up_process", task)
    ...
    (int)1

Arguments can be either [`Object`](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object")s or Python values. The function return value is returned as an [`Object`](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object"):

    >>> # GFP_KERNEL isn't in the kernel debug info
    >>> # We have to use this trick to get it.
    >>> for flag in prog["gfpflag_names"]:
    ...     if flag.name.string_() == b"GFP_KERNEL":
    ...             GFP_KERNEL = flag.mask
    ...             break
    ...
    >>> # kmalloc() is actually a macro.
    >>> # We have to call the underlying function.
    >>> p = call_function("__kmalloc_noprof", 13, GFP_KERNEL)
    >>> p
    (void *)0xffff991701ef43c0
    >>> identify_address(p)
    'slab object: kmalloc-16+0x0'
    >>> call_function("kfree", p)
    (void)<absent>
    >>> identify_address(p)
    'free slab object: kmalloc-16+0x0'

Variadic functions are also supported:

    >>> call_function("_printk", "Hello, world! %d\n", Object(prog, "int", 1234))
    (int)18
    >>> os.system("dmesg | tail -1")
    [ 1138.223004] Hello, world! 1234

Constructed values can be passed by pointer using [`pass_pointer()`](#drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.pass_pointer "drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.pass_pointer"):

    >>> sb = prog["init_fs"].root.mnt.mnt_sb
    >>> sb.s_shrink.scan_objects
    (unsigned long (*)(struct shrinker *, struct shrink_control *))super_cache_scan+0x0 = 0xffffffffbda4c487
    >>> sc = pass_pointer(Object(prog, "struct shrink_control",
    ...                   {"gfp_mask": GFP_KERNEL, "nr_to_scan": 100, "nr_scanned": 100}))
    >>> call_function(sb.s_shrink.scan_objects, sb.s_shrink, sc)
    (unsigned long)31

If the function modifies the passed value, the [`pass_pointer`](#drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.pass_pointer "drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.pass_pointer") object is updated:

    >>> sc.object
    (struct shrink_control){
            .gfp_mask = (gfp_t)3264,
            .nid = (int)0,
            .nr_to_scan = (unsigned long)1,
            .nr_scanned = (unsigned long)100,
            .memcg = (struct mem_cgroup *)0x0,
    }

Note

It is not possible to call some functions, including inlined functions and function-like macros. If the unavailable function is a wrapper around another function, sometimes the wrapped function can be called instead.

Warning

Calling a function incorrectly may cause the kernel to crash or misbehave in various ways.

The function is called from process context. Note that the function may have context, locking, or reference counting requirements.

Parameters:  
- **prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

- **func** – Function to call. May be a function name, function object, or function pointer object.

- **args** – Function arguments. [`int`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.14)"), [`float`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#float "(in Python v3.14)"), and [`bool`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#bool "(in Python v3.14)") arguments are converted as “literals” with `Object(prog,`` ``value=...)`. [`str`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.14)") and [`bytes`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#bytes "(in Python v3.14)") arguments are converted to `char` array objects. [`pass_pointer`](#drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.pass_pointer "drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.pass_pointer") arguments are copied to the kernel, passed by pointer, and copied back.

Returns:  
Function return value.

Raises:  
- [**TypeError**](https://docs.python.org/3/library/exceptions.html#TypeError "(in Python v3.14)") – if the passed arguments have incorrect types for the function

- [**ObjectAbsentError**](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.ObjectAbsentError "drgn.ObjectAbsentError") – if the function cannot be called because it is inlined

- [**LookupError**](https://docs.python.org/3/library/exceptions.html#LookupError "(in Python v3.14)") – if a function with the given name is not found (possibly because it is actually a function-like macro)

&nbsp;

drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.call_functions(*prog: [drgn.Program](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Program "drgn.Program")*, *calls: [Sequence](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Sequence "(in Python v3.14)")\[[Sequence](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Sequence "(in Python v3.14)")\[[Any](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Any "(in Python v3.14)")\]\]*) → [None](https://docs.python.org/3/library/constants.html#None "(in Python v3.14)")  
Call multiple functions in the kernel.

This is more efficient than calling [`call_function()`](#drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.call_function "drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.call_function") multiple times because it only inserts one kernel module.

    >>> leaked_namespaces
    [Object(prog, 'struct net *', value=0xffff8f568869af80), ...]
    >>> call_functions([("netns_put", ns) for ns in leaked_namespaces])

Function return values are ignored. Use [`call_function()`](#drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.call_function "drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.call_function") if you need the return value.

Warning

See [`call_function()`](#drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.call_function "drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.call_function") for warnings about calling kernel functions.

Parameters:  
- **prog** – Program, which [may be omitted to use the default program argument](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#default-program).

- **calls** – List of calls, where each call is a tuple of `(func,`` ``*args)`. See [`call_function()`](#drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.call_function "drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.call_function") for details on *func* and *args*.

&nbsp;

*class *drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.pass_pointer(*object: [Any](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Any "(in Python v3.14)")*)  
Wrapper used to pass values to [`call_function()`](#drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.call_function "drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.call_function") or [`call_functions()`](#drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.call_functions "drgn.helpers.experimental.kmodify.call_functions") by pointer.

Parameters:  
**object** – [`Object`](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object") or Python value to wrap.

object*: [Any](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.Any "(in Python v3.14)")*  
Wrapped object. Updated to an [`Object`](https://drgn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api_reference.html#drgn.Object "drgn.Object") containing the final value after the function call.
